‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Producer Alex Gayner Thought He’d Solved Every Problem a Set Could Throw at Him. Then Came a Runaway Parrot.

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Ten seconds of footage ended up being the most nerve-wracking part of the shoot for the movie veteran who has been on sets since the '90s.

Filmmakers often thrive on controlled chaos. Alex Gayner has spent his career orchestrating that madness.

Long before producing The Mosquito Bowl for Peter Berg, Gayner cut his teeth doing odd jobs on ’90s direct-to-video actioners — the kind of movies you’d stumble across on a pay-cable channel at 1 AM during a bout of insomnia. Then, a second AD was let go before lunch, and Gayner found himself promoted on the spot. It was an unlikely break that set the course for the rest of his career.

He gradually climbed the ranks to first assistant director, eventually trading B-movies for some of the biggest studio productions around, including Guillermo del Toro’s Pacific Rim and Gareth Edwards’ Godzilla. He also developed a reputation as a closer, overseeing reshoots for Suicide Squad (2016), Birds of Prey, Zero Dark Thirty and Mulan, among others. Related Stories TV 'Beef' Creator Lee Sung Jin Launches Production Company, Plans Ali Wong Limited Series Movies Ray Nicholson Levels Up With 'The Mosquito Bowl': "I'm Always Battling for Legitimacy"

Another turning point arrived in the late 2010s, when the native New Yorker decided to pivot to producing. If the first AD is the on-set quarterback who executes the plays on the field, then Gayner wanted to become a full-time offensive coordinator, the person drawing up the plays and mapping out the production behind the scenes.

He credits Nicolas Winding Refn with giving him his big break as a producer on the 2019 limited series Too Old to Die Young, an opportunity that paved the way for him to produce the first season of Beef and win an Emmy for his work. (Gayner and Refn are now assembling the latter’s long-gestating Maniac Cop movie, which recently received approval for a California tax credit.)

After wrapping Beef, Netflix pointed Gayner toward Berg’s 19th-century Western limited series American Primeval. But first, the two had to bridge the gap between the producer Berg expected and the one Gayner turned out to be.

“When I met Pete, I don’t think he really knew what kind of producer I was,” Gayner tells The Hollywood Reporter. “Naturally, he didn’t know if I was going to come in to watch the money or try to make the show on soundstages. We had to figure each other out.”

To keep the football metaphors going, Berg likes to run his sets with a no-huddle offense, frequently calling audibles along the way. He might improvise dialogue, change camera positions or throw out the playbook altogether. Most of all, he has a strict devotion to immersive, practical filmmaking. Fortunately, Gayner has been adapting on the fly ever since that lunchtime promotion more than 30 years ago. He also shares Berg’s preference for capturing as much as possible in camera.

Thus, they quickly got on the same page for American Primeval, setting the stage for two features, The Mosquito Bowl and the highly anticipated Call of Duty video game adaptation.

“That [adherence to practical] really solidified our relationship as filmmaking partners. He knew I wasn’t afraid to go back to the old-school way of how we learned to do things,” Gayner says. “It’s a little uncomfortable to get that realism and that immersive, visceral experience on film, but it’s worth pushing the envelope.”

The Mosquito Bowl, based on a novel by Berg’s cousin Buzz Bissinger, chronicles four real-life college football players who enlisted in the U.S. Marine Corps during World War II and played in a fabled 1944 football game known as the Mosquito Bowl alongside 60 other former collegiate players turned soldiers.

Berg and Gayner threw Nicholas Galitzine, Bill Skarsgård, Ray Nicholson and Tom Francis into football crash courses with help from San Francisco 49ers All-Pro running back Christian McCaffrey, the New York Giants and two of the industry’s best on-set football coordinators. Then they dropped the actors into the ocean and jungle of Queensland, Australia, doubling for Japan, while surrounding them with tanks, explosions, flamethrowers and blank gunfire.

Berg wanted to honor one specific character detail that belonged to Nicholson’s Tony Butkovich, the former Purdue University fullback who was drafted in the first round by the Cleveland Rams prior to enlistment. Bissinger wrote about how Butkovich sent photos home of himself with a parrot on his shoulder, so Gayner received the unenviable task of finding what would become the one-and-only available parrot on the island continent. But the bird was still young, and its wings had to be clipped to prevent catastrophe during filming.

After months of planning and training, cameras finally rolled on Nicholson and the supposedly grounded parrot. Within seconds, the parrot flew off into the 100-foot-tall trees. Its four wranglers panicked, as did the rest of the stunned cast and crew. Gayner, meanwhile, tried to counter Berg’s fatalism with optimism.

“Pete looked at me and said, ‘I know exactly what’s going to happen. The headline of every entertainment paper in L.A. is going to be, “Peter Berg Kills Parrot During Filming of The Mosquito Bowl.”’ And I said, ‘No, Pete, we’re going to find the parrot.’”

Hours later, they managed to retrieve the parrot from a tree and tie a string to its leg as an added precaution until they got the necessary shots. The ordeal amounted to roughly ten seconds of footage in the final cut, but it’s a stark reminder of how much labor goes into even the briefest moments in a film.

“We can control actors, special effects and stunts. But when the only parrot in Australia that’s available for our movie flies away, you’re stuck,” Gayner says. “We didn’t want to use a CG parrot. We’d made that commitment.”

Below, in a conversation with THR, Gayner elaborates on training two British actors and a Swedish actor to play a sport they’d never played before, let alone understood.

Your filmography is overwhelming. You’ve seen some things, haven’t you?

(Laughs.) I’ve seen a few things, yeah. I’ve been around the block. I’ve gone from tiny little things to big giant things — and everything in between.

I can only imagine the war stories you must have, especially as first assistant director. That’s widely considered the most stressful job on set. First ADs are often regarded as the quarterback to the director’s head coach. Did you have any kind of support group with your fellow first ADs?

Not really, because first ADs never work with other first ADs. You can talk to each other, share experiences and discuss your approaches to working with directors, but you never really get to spend time with them, especially on a movie set, unless there’s a first and second unit. Everybody has such different styles. But it is a stressful job. It’s a heavy-lifting job.

I started making films in Hollywood when I was 20 years old, and I worked for a very low-budget film company that made straight-to-video action movies. They filmed one movie every three weeks. So we would do 18-day shoots — three consecutive six-day weeks — with the exact same crew. We’d wrap a movie on a Friday, and then we’d start a new movie on a Monday. You’d get the script midway through one movie, and everybody would just roll onto the next film. I did that for three years, and I made 30 films in three years.

I did everything from driving the grip truck to learning how to load film, because we were still shooting film at the time, to being a boom person and working with the grips and the electric. So it really was my film school, and it taught me so much about what everybody does on a film set. Then I just happened to fall into the AD department because a second AD got let go. At lunch, they were like, “Alright, you’re now the second 2nd.” And I was like, “Okay, I’ll figure out how to do that.” Eventually, that happened with the [first] AD.

Why do you think these assistant director roles fit you so well?

I have a knack for communicating with all the departments and rallying everybody to have a common vision. And as I started to become a first AD on smaller movies before eventually doing some second-unit action sequences and working my way up to the bigger studio films, I made it less stressful for myself. I really understood what everybody was up against and what everybody was doing. I didn’t want to live under that constant pressure of only being focused on, How are we going to make our day? How are we going to get the schedule done? We have to just keep going.

That is part of the job, but really, I’m a filmmaker. I want to participate creatively in supporting the story and the characters. And as a first AD, if you can figure out how to do your job and motivate the crew — while working in a respectful environment with a couple hundred people, keeping them on the same page and being a good creative partner to the director, the producer and the studio — it takes a lot of the pressure off. You’re not being this machine butting up against an artist trying to birth something that’s in their brain.

So, as a first AD, I was always a creative first, and I figured out how to translate that into the schedule, the days, the shot lists and the time that the DP had to light. Integrating myself as a creative partner to everybody made it less stressful and more enjoyable.

Another pattern in your work is that you tend to assistant-direct a lot of additional photography. Examples include: Suicide Squad, Birds of Prey, Zero Dark Thirty, Mulan and Total Recall. That’s a fraught period of time since the production is spending the last of its money, and there’s a lot riding on the additions and/or revisions. Was there a reason why your number kept getting called for reshoots? Are you the Winston Wolf of reshoots?

(Laughs.) I don’t really know the answer. Part of it was schedule and circumstance. Another part of it was word of mouth. I really took pride in what I did as a first AD, and I’m a very even, mild-mannered first AD. There are some who are not. Everybody has their own style, but I always was very balanced and very calm in the pocket, so to speak, to go back to your quarterback reference. So I think that reputation just got around town.

Also, if a director’s usual first AD wasn’t available for reshoots, I tended to be on the short list to be called. It was very flattering. It was a really great way to build relationships with other directors without having to commit a year or two to the overall process, and it expanded my résumé quite a bit.

You’d produced here and there prior to 2023, but did your Emmy for producing Beef season one lead you to produce full-time? It looks like you stopped assistant-directing that same year.

I’d been going back and forth before producing Beef. The last movie I AD’d was Spiderhead with Joe Kosinski during COVID. It was a very hard transition. I’d worked so long to be successful as an AD, and then I looked in the mirror and went, “Alright, I’m going to stop doing this and make the transition to producer.” With a family and a mortgage, it was a big commitment to step into a new role, essentially starting at the bottom. But I knew that it was the natural next step, something I had to do and really wanted to do. The project that got me really confident as a producer was a limited series called Too Old to Die Young that Nicolas Winding Refn directed.

Big fan. Every time I see Nell Tiger Free, I remind her how haunting her beach scene with Miles Teller is.

I absolutely remember that day. We went up to the [Mugu] rock at the end of Malibu, right on the turn there. It was a wild show, and I loved making it. Nick originally called me to AD the series, and I turned him down because I was really committed to producing. But he kept calling me and calling me. And I said, “Nick, look, I really appreciate it, but I have to stick to this. I have to make this transition. This is what I’m going to do.” In 2017, limited series really weren’t that big a thing yet, and Amazon, under Roy Price, was also still finding its legs. This was long before Jen Salke came in.

Then Nick said, “I’ll tell you what, I’m going to direct this entire series by myself, which I’ve never done before. If you AD the first episode of the show for me, I’d love for you to produce the series. Once you get me on my feet, then you can produce the rest of the episodes. I just need you to prep for me and help me with casting and finding locations.”

So we figured out what would work for him and what would work for me, and we made a deal. I AD’d the first three weeks or so, and then I stepped off the set. Other ADs then came in, and I produced the rest of the show. Nick wrote the series with Ed Brubaker, and besides directing all the episodes, he was editing every night. So he was at it seven days a week, 20 hours a day.

It was a fantastic experience and one of the most creative things I’ve ever been a part of. It was such a specific vision that he had. We shot most of it in L.A. over a hundred days. It was a very small-budget show, and we stretched every dollar. That was really the beginning of producing for me.

Do you think Maniac Cop is ever going to happen?

I’m as sure as I can be. It just qualified for the California Tax Credit. It got approved. He’s making it as a film instead of a series. We tried to make it as a series for HBO. We were very close to making it, but once COVID hit, it was just impossible to make that kind of show at the time.

You wrapped Beef season one in 2022. In 2023, you EP’d American Primeval for Pete Berg. Did you hit it off to such a degree that he wanted you to be his boots-on-the-ground producer and UPM for The Mosquito Bowl?

American Primeval was a very complicated show to make out in the elements for a hundred-plus days. I was asked to come in as a non-writing EP. Pete and I had never worked together. I’d never even met him. I had previously worked with the other executive producer, Eric Newman, and I had a longstanding relationship with a lot of people at Netflix.

When I met Pete, I don’t think he really knew what kind of producer I was. He’s worked with a lot of the same people over the years, and he has this very raw, immersive, visceral vision of whatever he’s making. So, naturally, he didn’t know if I was going to come in to watch the money or try to make the show on soundstages. We had to figure each other out.

But as soon as we hit the ground in New Mexico, he learned that I have the same desire to make films and television as practically as he does. There are definitely benefits to going on stage and shooting blue screen for certain pieces, but for a huge sequence at the end that takes place on top of a snowy mountain, there was talk of going to a quarry-type setting and making fake snow in a very controlled environment. As soon as I started talking creatively with Pete about it, I knew there was no way this was going to work for what he was trying to do visually and what Mark L. Smith wrote.

My first reaction and suggestion to that proposed plan was, “I don’t want to do that. I want to gather a lean crew with as little gear as possible, and climb up a mountain in the real snow. We can build a shack and do it for real, while having access and keeping everybody as safe and comfortable as humanly possible.” To fabricate all that in the New Mexico summer and then add visual effects, it would’ve felt too different. Paper snow doesn’t react to horses the same way as real snow, and that went for every element of that sequence.

So that [adherence to practical filmmaking] really solidified our relationship as filmmaking partners. He knew I wasn’t afraid to go back to the old-school way of how we learned to do things. It’s a little uncomfortable to get that realism and that immersive, visceral experience on film, but it’s worth pushing the envelope.

Pete is known for having a very vocal, energetic and off-the-cuff directing style. He’ll throw out alternate dialogue mid-take without cutting. How did you adapt your own process to his “no-huddle” manner of shooting a movie?

I didn’t have to adapt that much. Working with Pete as a producer, it’s like creating an action-packed play. He’s very improvisational. If something comes to mind on set, he goes for it. Being a First AD for so many different kinds of directors, as well as shooting additional photography, really served me as a producer. I learned how to step into a preexisting structure, whether for a couple of scenes or a whole sequence, and adapt to a crew that’s already been through battle together. As a producer, I try to be as responsible for the active environment I’m creating as I am for the story.

Pete especially likes a 360-degree environment, and that’s how I approach every project. There’s no cheating. You can’t go out to a location with all your trucks lined up behind you and say, “Okay, you can only look west.” That 360 approach really slimmed everything down, allowing us to be nimble and giving Pete the freedom to say, “Okay, I want to turn around and look the opposite way.” It helped us discover some magic on American Primeval and The Mosquito Bowl. Organic pieces of the puzzle fell into place. The actors appreciated it, too.

Mosquito Bowl has numerous war and football sequences, which must have been a logistical nightmare. You weren’t the First AD on this set, but that experience must have served you well.

100 percent. Every challenging project I’ve produced has been made better by my experience and the tools I gained as a First AD. The Mosquito Bowl wasn’t a logistical nightmare, but it was very challenging logistically. There were so many challenges to figure out on a daily basis that I had to use every tool in my toolbox.

When Pete started writing and talking about The Mosquito Bowl while we were shooting American Primeval, we agreed that if we were going to make a war movie, we had to do something different. So many amazing World War II films have already been made. We had to take responsibility for trying to capture even 0.1 percent of what these soldiers went through emotionally. Obviously, we would never compare what we do as filmmakers to what any soldier experienced in war, but we had to deliver something immersive, violent and shocking for the audience while remaining true to what really happened.

That meant putting the actors in the ocean instead of in a water tank or a pool and using real explosions and blank gunfire instead of visual effects. We had to find running tanks and develop hundred-foot flamethrowers. As a producer, I was never going to let “it’s impossible” come out of my mouth. There was always going to be a way to make it as real as possible, and that became our mission statement from the beginning.

With every person we interviewed and every department we approached about joining The Mosquito Bowl, we said, “This is how we want to do it. We want to make this as real as possible. Can you do this? Are you up for it? And what are your ideas to help us get there?”

Did San Francisco 49ers running back Christian McCaffrey consult you guys as far as how to train the actors to look like capable football players in just four weeks?

He absolutely did. Christian McCaffrey helped us build the foundation for the actors to become football players. Pete, who’s friendly with Christian, gave him a call once we had Nicholas Galatzine, Bill Skarsgård, Tom Francis and Ray Nicholson on board. Christian is a beast. He’s one of the most amazing football players there is, and he’s such a gracious guy. He does so much work with veterans, and he’s involved with a nonprofit, so he was immediately willing to donate his time. He was even coming back from an injury at the time, but two days after Pete called him, he was on the field in Westlake with Ray, running plays, teaching him moves and consulting as a running back. Additionally, we sent Tom Francis to the New York Giants because he was doing Sunset Boulevard on Broadway, so he got to train with them.

We had two Brits, a Swede and one American, and with Ray being the American, he was the only one who had ever really played football. He played high school football and knew a lot about the game, but Nick, Tom and Bill didn’t know much about it. So they worked tirelessly with our football coordinators, Mike Fisher and Don Powers, who have worked on a lot of football movies. They came down to Australia and ran a four-week camp with the actors, bringing in a ton of Aussie rules football and rugby players, as well as a few college football players from the States to fill in at quarterback.

For all the football and battle sequences, ten seconds of a parrot interacting with Ray’s character was the task that put you through your paces the most?

(Laughs.) That put Pete through his paces more than anything else on the movie. I don’t know about myself. Every single day, my brain was working at a hundred miles an hour trying to solve problems. Birds and animals in Australia are different from those in other parts of the world. You have some of the deadliest snakes and spiders, as well as bull sharks. Everywhere you go, you have to be aware of nature’s pitfalls. So finding movie animals there was difficult, and Australia doesn’t have every breed you might find in North America or elsewhere.

Ray’s character, Tony Butkovich, really had a parrot as a pet during World War II. That was based on real information. But finding a movie-trained parrot that looked the way we wanted was nearly impossible. We worked with animal wranglers who eventually found one, but it was very young. They had to train it so we could feed it.

They also clipped its swings so it wouldn’t fly away. We had to shoot the scene within a specific window before the parrot got old enough to learn how to fly. So we were up against the deadline of the parrot instinctively knowing how to fly. We also shot the whole movie in continuity, so our first AD, Andrew Ward, had to figure out how to fit the scene into that window.

There had been months of logistical planning and training, and Ray had to understand what he could and couldn’t do with it. Eventually, the day came when we had to work with the parrot. We were in the jungle, surrounded by smoke. Everyone was carrying guns and wearing all this gear, but everything had to be silent and still.

Gosh, all this for ten seconds of footage.

Yeah, when Ray sat down to do the scene, the set had to be cleared. Pete couldn’t call action, and nobody could say a word. The animal wranglers put the parrot down next to Ray and made hand signals for the cameras to start rolling. Ray started feeding the parrot, and within two seconds, it flew off into the jungle. (Laughs.) The parrot that wasn’t supposed to be able to fly with its clipped wings disappeared up into the trees.

The animal wranglers freaked out, and we all had to stop and look up. We were looking for a tiny parrot in the Australian jungle, surrounded by hundred-foot trees and dense foliage. The animal wranglers then crawled through the jungle, making parrot noises and trying to call it back.

Pete looked at me and said, “I know exactly what’s going to happen. The headline of every entertainment paper in L.A is going to be, ‘Peter Berg Kills Parrot During Filming of The Mosquito Bowl.’” And I said, “No, Pete, we’re going to find the parrot.” So the search went on for hours, and the animal wranglers stacked ladders and held pool cues with baskets on the ends. Eventually, the parrot came back and we got the shots, but it was one of the most stressful ordeals.

We can control actors, special effects and stunts. We can have that kind of input. But when the only parrot in Australia that’s available for our movie flies away, you’re stuck. We didn’t want to use a CG parrot. We’d made that commitment.

You just proved why I’m endlessly fascinated by behind-the-scenes stories. Of all the projects you’ve been a part of, which one deserves the I Play Rocky treatment?

It’s between American Primeval and Too Old to Die Young, but it’s hard to pick one or the other because they’re very different.

American Primeval was logistically the hardest project I’ve ever produced and made. We did it for real in the New Mexico desert through winter, spring, summer and fall, with no cover sets and no place to go, with horses and 1800s footwear and clothing, a writers’ strike and a SAG strike. Taylor Kitsch, our lead actor, even broke his foot, and Betty Gilpin, our lead actress, got pregnant during the strikes before having to come back to finish the show. We went from shooting in one winter season with the heaviest snow in New Mexico to finishing the show the next winter season, and it was New Mexico’s warmest winter with no snow.

Too Old to Die Young was the most eccentric creative experience I’ve ever produced. Nick shot 10-minute-long takes where the camera didn’t move. We drove around Hollywood at three in the morning with John Hawkes and Miles Teller. They drove a police car by themselves up the 405 while I was in the backseat with a camera in my hand. A CHP officer followed us and allowed us to do that night after night. It was the most surreal experience.

***The Mosquito Bowl is now streaming on Netflix.

Gasperini: ‘Roma knew 2nd half would be decisive’ against PSG

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Gian Piero Gasperini is ‘happy and proud’ that his Roma gamble paid off with a 2-2 Champions League draw away to PSG. ‘If we stayed in the game, the second half would be decisive.’

The Giallorossi were the underdogs going into Parc des Princes, against the side that has dominated the Champions League in the last two years and had won all four of their games so far this season.

There were some huge surprises in the starting XI, as Malen, Paulo Dybala and Bryan Cristante were all on the bench, while Niccolò Pisilli was picked behind Santiago Castro to follow Vitinha around.

“I am very happy and proud of this evening’s performance. It was obvious we would not change our approach even against the best team, and PSG are certainly the best team in Europe right now,” confessed Gasperini on Sky Sport Italia.

“They not only have a lot of talent at their disposal, but also great organisation and a variety of different systems they can call upon. It was a very important match for us, but I will watch it back, because there was a lot to learn from it that can help us going forward in Serie A and the Champions League.”

Nonetheless, Mario Hermoso gave them the early lead after five minutes, turned around by Illia Zabarnyi’s free header on a corner and the Ousmane Dembele penalty, until substitute Donyell Malen converted another spot-kick for the Vitinha handling offence.

Was it because Roma have shown a tendency to fall away in the final 20 minutes of European games with this very tiring style of football, so he wanted their quality off the bench?

“Malen has played many games, including international duty, so it was only fair to give Castro a chance. It’s also true that the plan was to introduce him in the second half, which is when the games are decided,” explained Gasperini.

“Dybala had also made his comeback against Parma after more than a month out, so he needed to be looked after. Cristante played a lot too, but I introduced Pisilli, who was fantastic. It’s not as if these players were replaced by someone inferior.

“We knew that if we stayed in the game, then the second half would become decisive. PSG introduced Dembele and other players of the highest quality, so for a while the game was going our way, and at the end we couldn’t wait for the final whistle to come.”

When Fabio Capello read the starting XI, he wondered if Gasperini and Roma were surrendering the Champions League to focus on Serie A, but there was a plan behind it.

“I am particularly satisfied tonight,” laughed Gasperini.

“I knew there were some risks, but not for me, because I see these lads improving, and if we can get through games like this, it’s a big boost for everyone.”

Soulé was a player who seemed to be on the way out of Roma last season, but has instead managed to go from strength to strength.

“Along with being a strong player, he is also a very positive guy who learns quickly and wants to improve. He is only 23 years old, is much more complete now that he used to be, as he played on the left, behind the two strikers, whereas before he wouldn’t leave his little area.

“He is achieving maturity now, and I am so happy for him, because it’s been a couple of months that he has really progressed. My job is to get the best out of these players, but I cannot add characteristics that they do not have, I just need to help them to flourish,” assured Gasperini.

Finally, he was asked if Roma are focused more on Serie A or the Champions League?

Europe's Hera mission returns to the chaotic scene of a cosmic smashup

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

“DART made a really, really large mess. However, we didn’t really know what kind of mess.”

Four years ago, NASA dispatched a small spacecraft on a suicide mission to show how humanity might deflect an asteroid on a collision course with Earth. The first-of-its-kind experiment worked beautifully as the solar-powered space probe slammed into asteroid Dimorphos, permanently altering its trajectory.

Using telescopes, astronomers measured changes in the orbit of Dimorphos around a larger asteroid companion named Didymos in the aftermath of NASA’s Double Asteroid Redirection Test (DART) mission. The spacecraft released some 10 billion joules of energy as it impacted Dimorphos in September 2022 at more than 13,700 mph (6.14 kilometers per second), shortening the asteroid’s 12-hour orbit around Didymos by about 33 minutes, 25 times the most conservative expectation.

But scientists could only learn so much from DART. The collision destroyed the spacecraft, as expected. DART recorded images as it took aim on Dimorphos, but only faraway telescopes and a small CubeSat that briefly detected the impact flash could see what happened next.

“We knew right away that the impact was successful, that DART made a really, really large mess,” said Sabina Raducan, a planetary scientist at the International Space Science Institute in Bern, Switzerland. “However, we didn’t really know what kind of mess. We were still missing pieces of the puzzle.”

The European Space Agency’s Hera spacecraft recently arrived in the vicinity of Didymos and Dimorphos after a two-year journey from a launch pad in Florida. On Wednesday, ESA released Hera’s first views of the scar left by DART on Dimorphos. The images show the car-sized impactor carved out a crater the size of a stadium, nearly as big as Dimorphos itself, a rugged chunk of extraterrestrial rock measuring about 500 feet (151 meters) across.

Mission planners selected Didymos and Dimorphos for the deflection experiment because they are similar to many near-Earth asteroids, but there’s no risk of them striking our home planet. Scientists weren’t sure what Hera would find when it got to the double asteroid, currently orbiting the Sun about 120 million miles (193.5 million kilometers) from Earth.

“I would say we had two kinds of predictions: either we made a crater, or we reshaped the asteroid,” said Patrick Michel, Hera’s principal investigator from Côte d’Azur Observatory in Nice, France. “In fact, we did both because the crater is so large that the shape has changed. Therefore, we are in a sort of a transition between just cratering and global transformation, and we are still not sure how this can happen.”

Hera found Didymos, the larger of the two asteroids, spinning on its axis just as DART observed on final approach in 2022. Dimorphos is no longer so stable.

“As Hera returned more images, it became clear that the DART impact has left Dimorphos tumbling wildly in its orbit,” says Michael Kueppers, ESA’s Hera mission scientist.

The Hera spacecraft will fly closer to Didymos and Dimorphos over the next six months to obtain more detailed observations. Hera will release two small sub-satellites to attempt landings on Dimorphos and collect data on the asteroid’s composition, internal structure, and mass.

Scientists will use this information to reconstruct the dynamics of DART’s impact four years ago and better understand how a future deflection mission might move an asteroid off a trajectory that actually threatens Earth. Near-Earth asteroids come in many shapes and sizes, with different densities and makeups, so knowledge of Dimorphos’s physical properties will anchor predictive computer models teaching scientists how best to deflect all types of asteroids.

“Once we understand, we have a breakthrough, which really solves planetary defense,” Michel said in an interview with Ars.

In its initial reconnaissance this month, Hera spotted hundreds of boulders surrounding the double asteroid. This was not entirely unexpected. A picture taken by the Hubble Space Telescope shortly after DART’s impact showed a cloud of material streaming behind Didymos and Dimorphos, but scientists were astonished by the extent of the debris swarm in Hera’s first images.

“As we approached Didymos and Dimorphos, we quickly realized the asteroids were not alone,” said Naomi Murdoch, planetary scientist at the Institut Supérieur de l’Aéronautique et de l’Espace in Toulouse, France. “In fact, they had a lot of company.”

An image released by ESA on Wednesday combines 11 long exposure frames from Hera’s hyperspectral camera. Image processors on the ground digitally removed the relatively bright Didymos and Dimorphos to reveal a panoply of dimmer streaks and dots, each of which is a rock fragment ranging in size from a football to a washing machine, Murdoch said.

“Hera came to visit two asteroids, and we found hundreds of companions,” Murdoch said.

One obvious explanation for the boulders is they were thrown off Dimorphos when DART slammed into it. But scientists cautioned they need more information before they can say that with surety. Hera is the first space mission to undertake a sustained study of an asteroid with its own moon. The debris around the two asteroids might be relics from the creation of Dimorphos some time in the ancient past.

“Hera will allow us to study that in more detail,” Murdoch said. “We really need to know more about these boulders and characterize them to understand if they’re linked to DART, if they’re linked to another process, and what the consequences might be for other asteroid systems in the Solar System.”

The boulder field will create challenges for navigating around the asteroids. The boulders are an added risk for the two small CubeSats Hera is supposed to deploy in January.

“We saw a couple of images that didn’t reassure me,” said Margherita Cardi, project manager for the Milani CubeSat. “There is a mix of excitement and panic.”

Claudia Balossi, spacecraft systems engineer for Hera’s other CubeSat, named Juventas, said her mission’s planned trajectory would take it right through the boulders as it moves within a couple of miles of the asteroids, then finally toward a landing attempt on Dimorphos, where it will measure the miniature world’s tenuous gravity field.

“We are right in the middle of it,” Balossi said. “The smallest ones are probably just as big as we are and the biggest ones are very big. In the worst case, a boulder will land on us. We will activate our gravimeter right away [if that happens],” Balossi joked.

The good news is the asteroids’ gravity is so weak that Hera, Milani, Juventas, and the hazards around them all move at snail-like speeds. Hera’s current trajectory takes it around the asteroids at a relative velocity of just a quarter-mile per hour, or about 12 centimeters per second. At that speed, there’s no danger of a sudden catastrophic hypervelocity collision between the spacecraft and the boulders lurking nearby.

Scientists have seen this at an asteroid before. NASA’s OSIRIS-REx spacecraft kicked up an estimated six tons of rock and dust when it gently moved in to collect a sample from an asteroid named Bennu in 2020. Low gravity and weak surface cohesion are a recipe for outsized effects.

“These types of bodies, because of their low gravity, have a very counterintuitive behavior,” Michel said. “Each time we interact with them, whether to take a sample or to make an impact, the response of the surface was not the one we predicted, simply because our intuition relies on what we experience every day on Earth. Even if the rules of physics are the same in the Universe, they express very differently depending on local attraction.”

Ars Technica has been separating the signal from the noise for over 25 years. With our unique combination of technical savvy and wide-ranging interest in the technological arts and sciences, Ars is the trusted source in a sea of information. After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Who won the Aaron Rodgers-Mike McCarthy battle? Mike Tomlin

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

After Aaron Rodgers' benching Tuesday night, the Rodgers-Mike McCarthy relationship reached critical mass Wednesday when the quarterback unleashed a torrent of accusations against his former head coach.

Among them: Rodgers said McCarthy didn't watch tape, missed team meetings, slept through a few he attended and even said "1-2-3, Packers!" while breaking a team huddle. Shortly after Rodgers' revelatory appearance on "The Pat McAfee Show," the Steelers released Rodgers.

Rodgers is now left to pick up the pieces and decide his suddenly cloudy future in what we all believed would be his final NFL season. McCarthy might have the bigger immediate mess, left to coach his struggling team in a game against the 8-2 Broncos and face some incendiary accusations that tend to undercut a coach's authority.

But you know who isn't losing sleep right now? The guy who's set to unleash his latest Minecraft creation. Wednesday night's subject: "Ballparks, stadiums and varsity sports venues." How relatively quaint!

Mike Tomlin lives the dream right now. He revealed his 12-year secret about his Minecraft, and the world has accepted it with bemusement and wonder. Tomlin also has found strong footing on NBC as one of its Sunday night analysts, offering listeners a natural delivery, a willingness to give unvarnished opinions, solid coaching-style breakdowns and his now-patented "Tomlinisms."

And he's doing it all with a big smile on his face.

"Man, I'm having a ball, man," Tomlin said on the "New Heights" podcast earlier this season. "It's part-time work, full-time pay, you know what I mean?"

Tomlin has always had humor in his back pocket. He has also displayed pretty good timing over the years. Tomlin knew when to shift the emphasis away from the "Killer B's" in their later years, and he knew it was time to move on from malcontent players such as Antonio Brown, Martavis Bryant and Diontae Johnson.

Tomlin also knew when — and how — to gracefully exit the Steelers. No scorched-earth policy. No bridge burning or axe grinding. He also seemed to understand his place in everything and that it was time to walk away.

"Football, man, is bigger than all of us, and I never took myself so seriously that I thought that I was immune from that," Tomlin said in a recent episode of the "Not Just Football" podcast with Steelers defensive tackle Cameron Heyward. "I never wanted to stay too long."

Tomlin mentioned carrying "19 years of baggage" and how that could weigh down the Steelers.

"I just thought we had the makings of a really good team, a team that is capable of competing for and winning a championship," Tomlin said, "but when I really started thinking about it, man, my bags might slow that down."

Aaron Rodgers showered praise on Mike Tomlin during an interview Wednesday on ESPN.

This is not to suggest that Tomlin never experienced controversy or tough times with the Steelers. There have been plenty of each. But Tomlin has come out looking rosy amid all the current ugliness over at the Rooney Sports Complex.

Rodgers even made a point of praising Tomlin — as he did throughout their time together — in trashing McCarthy on Wednesday.

"We're all coming from Mike T, the polar opposite," Rodgers said. "Mike T, leader of men. He knew everybody's name, knew where they came from. That's what I told [McCarthy]: 'You don't have to be Mike T, but you have to connect with the guys.'"

Heck, even McCarthy has thrown roses in Tomlin's direction, saying recently: "I'm really happy for Mike. I'm trying not to laugh because I've never seen him smile so much. I'm told he's very happy. He's doing a great job."⁠

Sit back, unveil your Minecraft world, earn praise from both sides — two parties who despise each other — and collect some NBC paychecks. Not the worst gap year imaginable.

Tomlin can do whatever he wants next year: go back to coaching, keep doing the TV/Minecraft thing or try something new. Heck, would it shock anyone if some Steelers were pining for Tomlin and letting the powers that be know how they feel?

Interestingly, the Steelers still technically control Tomlin's rights, as he stepped down with two years left on his contract. The thinking in NFL circles is that the Steelers could receive compensation from another team interested in Tomlin's services, should he decide to take another job elsewhere. But now?

A reunion would still feel like a bridge too far. But the odds of Tomlin coming back to Pittsburgh, however minuscule they might be, are certainly higher today than they were a week ago. This is the power and position he's earned in light of all the darkness descending on the city.

There isn't a single person entangled in this Rodgers-McCarthy mess who comes out looking cleaner than Tomlin. Say what you will about Rodgers, but he's not exactly known for seeing eye-to-eye with every coach he's ever had. And perhaps there's some ulterior motive at play here, but it's hard to find Rodgers saying a bad word about Tomlin.

And that might not be viewed as a positive to everyone, Rodgers being who he is. Some McCarthy backers or Rodgersphiles might even take it as an indictment of Tomlin allowing Rodgers to manipulate him, or that Rodgers' undying praise is evidence for Tomlin being too much of a players' coach.

Even still, by doing nothing coaching-related this season, Tomlin has given us a refresher of why he was roundly respected as Steelers head coach — beyond even his unquestioned résumé topper of never having a losing season.

Tomlin is viewed as having established credibility and culture. He knew his players personally. He's also been known for his consistency and fairness, even with the toughest of personalities and controversies. It was easy to see how well respected Tomlin is by how many players greeted him on the field when NBC broadcast the Steelers game two weeks ago.

Many ex-Steelers praised Tomlin as a leader of men on his way out of the job, too. Some, like James Harrison, have criticized Tomlin's coaching, but it's not as easy to find prominent Steelers questioning Tomlin's character on the record.

And now he gets to avoid this mess that's unfolding in front of our eyes. Tomlin walked away, wiped his hands of the situation and, by all accounts, appears to be living his best life. It might be tempting to tune into Tomlin's second episode Wednesday night, not only to witness his career respawn but also to see if he offers any new Tomlinisms, subtle breadcrumbs on his smoldering former franchise, or perhaps just a knowing look.

As Tomlin once famously said: "Don't blink. If you're a blinker, cut your eyelids off."

‘Oozing with confidence’ – Chris Sutton swoons over Liverpool star who won 6 duels v Club Brugge

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Chris Sutton has said that Florian Wirtz looks like a ‘different’ player for Liverpool this season compared to last.

The Germany playmaker endured plenty of criticism during his first year in English football following his £100m+ move from Bayer Leverkusen, and that continued into the early weeks of this campaign.

However, the 23-year-old has been excellent since the extended international window earlier in the autumn, and he netted the Reds’ second goal in their 3-0 victory away to Club Brugge on Wednesday night.

It’s the third consecutive game in which Liverpool’s number 7 has scored, and he now has four goals and five assists across his last 10 matches in all competitions for the Reds (Transfermarkt).

Speaking on BBC Radio 5 Live towards the end of the win in Belgium, Sutton was keen to praise Wirtz for his performance against Ivan Leko’s side.

The former Blackburn and Chelsea striker said: “It’s a different Florian Wirtz this season. He’s just oozing with confidence.”

Want more Empire of the Kop coverage? Add us as a preferred source on Google to your favourites list for news you can trust

The German’s prolific form of late is reminiscent of the purple patch that he enjoyed over the festive period last year and into January, when he scored six times in 10 matches.

It isn’t just his improvement in terms of goal contributions which has stood out, though. As Cody Gakpo rightly highlighted in recent weeks, the 23-year-old boasts ‘incredible football intelligence‘ and is now linking up brilliantly with his attacking teammates.

Liverpool Echo reporter Ian Doyle awarded Wirtz 8/10 in his post-match player ratings, writing that our number 7 ‘buzzed with real intent behind [Alexander] Isak’ and was ‘constantly first on to second balls, which he used well, whilst also netting ‘with aplomb’ for his goal.

The German completed two dribbles and won six duels against Club Brugge (Sofascore), and having found the confidence to match his ability of late, he’s now grown into one of the Reds’ best performers.

Those critics who were tripping over themselves to lay into the 23-year-old must now be feeling rather foolish as they watch him exert an ever-greater influence for Andoni Iraola’s side. Long may it continue!

FC Barcelona’s third defeat this season, in all competitions, will not have happened this Wednesday. And it’s no surprise since the Catalans were away to the very modest Sabah FC. Against the Azerbaijani club, Barça logically won their match on the 5th day of the Champions League league phase (1-4).

With Lamine Yamal diminished and absent from the match sheet, Hansi Flick lined up a somewhat reshuffled eleven. Gabriel Jesus, for example, started up front, while Jules Koundé started in the right lane. The locals courageously tried their luck at the start of the match and even found the crossbar on a strike from Ivan Lepinjica (27th).

But this episode past, it was the Catalans who gradually got their foot on the ball. Anthony Gordon ended up opening the scoring after half an hour of play (0-1, 31st). Two minutes later, the Englishman thought he had his double, but the VAR signaled an offside (33rd).

Regardless, the former Newcastle player was back at the start of the second half and made the break for Barça (0-2, 51st). Too complicated for Sabah FC who failed a third time with a goal from Karim Adeyemi (0-3, 55th). And a fourth when Gabriel Jesus found the fault in his turn (0-4, 60th).

From then on, the visitors took their foot off the gas. A boon for the locals who reduced the gap and saved the honor by Joy Mickels (1-4, 63rd). The end of the meeting will be soporific. Note, however, a new injury on the Barça side with the premature exit of Alejandro Balde (64th). This success allows the Blaugrana to temporarily move up to fourth place in the standings. Sabah is thirty-second.

Wirtz magic edges Liverpool closer to knockout stages

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

It had to be this way. Liverpool number sevens have historically defined matches against Bruges so how appropriate it was that Florian Wirtz should preserve that rich heritage.

The late Kevin Keegan thrashed home a free-kick at this stadium 50 years ago to win Liverpool the Uefa Cup, while Sir Kenny Dalglish was the hero at Wembley in 1978 when his deft finish ensured the European Cup was retained.

That jersey means a players keeps the best company, and while this fixture didn’t carry the meaning of those historic occasions of the past, it was symbolic that Wirtz, who has really found his rhythm, should decorate this contest and take Liverpool a step closer to the knockout phases.

On another night Alexander Isak would have expected his two goals to take the headlines but he would have appreciated more than anyone the magic spells that Wirtz kept casting and had a glorious view of the strike that essentially secured three points in the frozen north of Belgium.

The idea of Bruges playing in the biggest club fixture of all, sadly, seems fanciful now given how much the landscape has changed but this was an occasion the hosts were going to enjoy and they did their best to make Liverpool uncomfortable in the hectic opening exchanges.

“The most important thing is the three points. I would not say it was the best we have played this season, for phases we played well. It was not easy here and they put us under pressure. Maybe the score does not show what the game was but we are happy and we showed our quality in the end. It was important to get the second goal, which we wanted earlier in the second half, it took a while but it was an important goal.”

“It was a very important game for us and a key moment in this competition, that puts us in a good position. We have played very well in terms of being efficient as a team. It was a harder game than it looks now with the result. It was difficult to expect a clean sheet as it was going to be an open game and they throw men forward. There is great merit in keeping a clean sheet. Overall, a very good performance. The three goals were very nice. We had good transitions. Definitely the worst part of the game [Victor Munoz’s first-half injury]. He was in pain in the moment but now he is feeling better. Let’s hope it is not something big and hope it is just days.”

Ahead of the teams emerging, both sets of fans belt out You’ll Never Walk Alone at Jan Breydel Stadium. Not only did these sides meet in two European finals during the 1970s but also they both sing that anthem.

Rare are the occasions when both sets of fans can sing You’ll Never Walk Alone together but this is one of them. Liverpool’s anthem has been Bruges’s since the teams met in the 1978 European Cup final, having previously contested the 1976 UEFA Cup final. Liverpool, of course, won both and a feature has just been shown on Belgian TV from Bruges’s perspective of the defeat at Wembley. Incredibly, this is the first time the teams have faced each other since then and, as you can imagine, the atmosphere here is particularly boisterous.

Here come the Reds 👋 https://t.co/5PThv29NIY

Jürgen Klopp will not want any credit, but some seeds he planted have started to bloom. Similarly, Andoni Iraola will not ask for recognition, but his constant encouragement is reaping dividends.

Past and present do not always mix in football, yet in Florian Wirtz’s case, the manager who once took Liverpool to the summit of the game and the one who wants to return them to that exalted position in the future have proved to be the perfect blend.

Wirtz has been a hot topic of debate since he joined Liverpool, but the tone of the discussion has significantly changed. From looking like a lost tourist, the German is producing the artistry and output that encouraged Anfield’s recruitment to make him the club’s first £100m signing.

Sabah FK 0 Barcelona 3 (55’)Slavia Prague 0 Villarreal 0 (53’)Atletico Madrid vs Viking 8pmClub Brugge vs Liverpool 8pmInter Milan vs Stuttgart 8pmLille vs Bayern Munich 8pmPSG vs Roma 8pmShakhtar Donetsk vs Fenerbahce 8pmSporting vs Manchester United 8pm

Our headliners for tonight. 🔥 https://t.co/gpqfwbE4ZQ

Tonight's team 🔴💫 #UCL https://t.co/xDjG3RXeEv

Liverpool have travelled over to Belgium to take on Club Brugge on matchday five in the Champions League. These sides have met competitively three times before and they have all come in finals, resulting in famous nights for Liverpool. In 1976, Liverpool beat Club Brugge 4-3 on aggregate across two legs to win the Uefa Cup. Then, Kenny Dalglish scored the only goal of the game at Wembley back in May 1978 as Liverpool won the European Cup for the second straight season, becoming the first English side to do so.

Liverpool, who have won 13 of their previous 17 matches against Belgian sides, won their first three Champions League games, beating Atletico Madrid, LASK and Villarreal but lost 3-2 away at Fenerbahce on matchday four despite leading twice in that game. After tonight’s game in Belgium, Liverpool’s remaining games in the Champions League are against Porto, Inter Milan and Lens. Andoni Iraola’s side come into this game with a spring in their step after thrashing rivals Manchester United 4-0 at Anfield on Sunday.

“A win [against Manchester United on Sunday] is always great but you do not have time to celebrate with this schedule,” Iraola said. “By Monday, we were already watching analysis of Club Brugge. I have enjoyed analysing them [Brugge], they are very energetic and have had an incredible run of games. I think we will have to be at a very good level, otherwise it is going to be very difficult. They have players who can make the difference and obviously we do as well, so I expect quite an open game. We are both aggressive, both want to press, there will be spaces and hopefully we can win the one-vs-one battles.”

Club Brugge have made a strong start to this season’s Champions League; after losing their first game to Aston Villa, they have since won their next three, including wins over Inter Milan, Lens and PSV. That puts them on the same number of points as Liverpool and are just one place behind. In the Belgian Pro League at the weekend, they thrashed Mechelen 5-1 and they sit second in the Belgian top flight, one point behind Union SG.

“I am proud of the players and what they have achieved against Aston Villa, Inter, Lens and PSV,” Club Brugge boss Ivan Leko said. “We know we should be grateful to be here, but we really want to try to show that Belgian football is fun to watch and we can win matches. I am proud, but I know we are a big club. You are only as good as your last match. We know what happened in the past, so there is also a nice connection between both clubs and the supporters. It is a special fixture to play, but we are not here just to enjoy the Champions League; we want to win and that is what we are going to try to do tomorrow [tonight].”

Mike McCarthy responds to Aaron Rodgers, confirms QB gave him 'ultimatum:' 'I know how to win'

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

If not listed, please contact your TV provider.

In the wake of Aaron Rodgers' sudden benching, the now-former Pittsburgh Steeler went scorched Earth on coach Mike McCarthy, accusing him of everything from incompetence to negligence. Facing a litany of embarrassing allegations, McCarthy has now shared his side of the story.

McCarthy began his press conference by stating that he wouldn't reveal any specifics about his private conversations with Rodgers. Asked why McCarthy was the right person to lead Pittsburgh through this messy situation, McCarthy bluntly referred to his overall track record as a coach.

"I know how to win, and that's the facts," McCarthy replied. "This is my third program. I plan on winning here because of the men and because of this organization."

Explaining the decision to bench Rodgers, which ignited this firestorm, McCarthy said the quarterback gave him an "ultimatum." At that point, McCarthy felt the need to make a change.

"It was a process that went on throughout this week," McCarthy said. "We got to a point. We talk about communication and things like that. There was an ultimatum made clearly outside of his job description. So, it was my decision to bench him."

Rodgers claimed that no one informed him of McCarthy's decision to bench him, and he only found out when tight end Pat Freirmuth alerted him to the news. McCarthy refuted Rodgers' story, saying there were multiple attempts to reach him.

"I know there were continuous efforts to reach Aaron, and I know there was communication with his agent," McCarthy said. "I don't know exactly the specifics on the other side of the conversation."

Asked directly about Rodgers' accusation that McCarthy broke down a team huddle at halftime of Sunday's loss to the Eagles by saying, "1-2-3 Packers," McCarthy conceded that it was an "embarrassing moment." He cited working "long hours" as an explanation for the slip-up. However, McCarthy added that he and Rodgers had a "difference of opinion" about how the locker room received it.

Speaking of the locker room, Rodgers also alleged that McCarthy has made little effort to connect with the players, particularly those on defense. The Steelers coach said he met with the team's leadership council and felt like both sides came to an understanding.

"I'll just say that I came from a player council meeting," McCarthy said. "I thought it was a meeting that was very direct and very honest. I appreciated the opportunity to talk through numerous things that have been on their mind. Comments that brought discussion to the forefront. I'm proud of our leadership. More importantly, I loved the urgency coming out of that meeting."

McCarthy added that some of those things "should've been talked about earlier" and presented a "growth opportunity" for himself and the team.

"Probably should have had more dialogue earlier in the process when I first arrived here," McCarthy admitted. "I do respect Mike Tomlin and his career here and the things that are in place. I don't have a throw-the-baby-out-with-the-bathwater philosophy. When you have situations that create honest and direct conversation, there are good things that come out of that."

When the Steelers hired McCarthy to replace Mike Tomlin, one of the first things he did was advocate for the return of Rodgers at quarterback. At the time, McCarthy didn't hesitate when asked about the idea of re-signing the veteran quarterback.

"Definitely. I don't see why you wouldn't," McCarthy said. "I have spoken to Aaron, and so that's really where we are there. I was able to sit back and watch the games. I watched most of the Pittsburgh games on TV, and I thought he was a great asset for the team."

Once a McCarthy-Rodgers reunion was finalized, Pittsburgh hoped that duo had a little more magic left after their run to a Super Bowl XLV championship with the Green Bay Packers. Instead, the relationship has turned toxic in a matter of months.

It was bad enough when the offense was sputtering and Rodgers was arguing with McCarthy on the sideline, but that seems rather rosy compared to where things stand now. On his way out the door, Rodgers asserted that players don't respect McCarthy as Pittsburgh fights to stay in the AFC playoff race with Mason Rudolph under center.

In an appearance on "The Pat McAfee Show," Rodgers teed off on McCarthy and his alleged lack of professionalism since taking over in Pittsburgh.

First, Rodgers claimed he found out about his benching through tight end Pat Friermuth. In fact, Rodgers said McCarthy still hasn't spoken to him about the decision. From there, Rodgers asserted that McCarthy was an absentee coach in the lead-up to the Steelers' 19-16 loss to the Eagles.

"Last Wednesday, we were in a quarterback meeting," Rodgers said. "Mike came in late, sat down, and within 10 minutes began snoring. Woke himself up, was rattling his papers. We all looked around. Five minutes later, you hear the snoring again, and less than five minutes after that, he walks out of the room, like nothing happened. Thursday, he misses an offensive meeting.

"Friday, he doesn't come to the walkthrough. We only have a walkthrough really on Friday. Doesn't come to the first walkthrough. Doesn't come to protection walkthrough on Thursday. Saturday, he misses the morning meeting, and we don't have a team meeting Saturday night for whatever reason in Philly. And then comes to Sunday, when obviously we struggled, and the product wasn't great, and you know there were some guys early on that didn't like the way things were going, especially defensive guys that Mike didn't want to get another defense.

"So a lot of those guys, you know, he kind of already lost a lot of those guys. And I think the nail in the coffin for so many of us was at halftime."

Ultimately, Rodgers said the "nail in the coffin" came at halftime of the loss. The ex-Steelers quarterback said McCarthy went from a "rambling" speech to breaking the team down with, "1-2-3 Packers." At that moment, Rodgers said he felt McCarthy completely lost the locker room.

Now Pittsburgh is left preparing to play the Denver Broncos on a short week as the team tries to pick up the pieces from a humiliating Wednesday for the entire organization.

Google Adds Adorable Snoopy And Woodstock Easter Egg For Thanksgiving

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Every year on the fourth Thursday of November, people across the United States gather for the special harvest feast that is Thanksgiving. Some like to observe it with a simple dinner, others like to mix in the tradition of watching or playing a Thanksgiving football game, while others drink in the pop culture extravaganza that is the Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade, regardless if it's postponed on a given year. But for many in the US, Peanuts plays a special role in conveying messages of togetherness, friendship, and, of course, sharing what everyone is grateful for each year.

Now, it seems Google has gotten on board to celebrate the spirit of the holiday, Peanuts-style. Users based in the United States can open the main Google search engine homepage to see a special Doodle of an animated gif of Snoopy and Woodstock setting the table for the Thanksgiving feast. International users can see the Doodle HERE. It's certainly adorable, and may brighten the day for anyone looking to confirm the 100th Macy's Thanksgiving Day Parade's new date of Saturday, November 28, 2026 at 8:30 AM ET, for the sake of planning ahead. As it turns out, this particular Google Doodle isn't necessarily in the spirit of the dinner or even the weekend itself, but rather, the preparation that goes into it.

For many in the family, it might feel like traveling or setting aside time to spend with loved ones is enough logistical work that goes into Thanksgiving. But as Snoopy and Woodstock illustrate succinctly, it's often a task undertaken by far too few people any given year, while preparing a massive spread to enjoy together at the dinner table. The Google Doodle celebrates just that, the work that goes into setting up those same dinners each year, playing a small animated segment of the corresponding scenes from A Charlie Brown Thanksgiving. But for Peanuts fans, the weekend fun shouldn't stop there.

Will Ferrell Golf Comedy ‘The Hawk’ Canceled at Netflix After Just One Season

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

“The Hawk,” a Will Ferrell golf comedy that debuted just this summer, has been canceled by Netflix and will not return for a second season, an individual with knowledge told IndieWire.

Ferrell co-created the series in which he plays a former top golfer who is now trying to return to the tour. And while it figured to be a tentpole series for Netflix, one that carried possibly the most expensive budget for a comedy series on Netflix and reunited Ferrell with his “SNL” co-star Molly Shannon and co-creator Harper Steele, it didn’t connect with critics or audiences.

“The Hawk” spent just three weeks on Netflix’s Top 10 when it debuted back in July, topping out at #2 in its second week on Netflix with just 6.2 million views. Ferrell’s vulgar, loudly-dressed golfer with a personalized visor and big mess of bleached blonde hair played a lot like some of Ferrell’s other string of sports comedies throughout the years. But even our critic was perplexed by the series’ tone that makes it sometimes play like a “bizarre addiction drama.” Related Stories Where Are Rick Dalton and Sharon Tate in ‘The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth’? ‘The Mosquito Bowl’ Review: ‘Friday Night Lights’ Meets ‘Five Came Back’ in Peter Berg’s Formulaic Netflix Movie About a World War II Football Game

Steele, in a recent appearance on “Late Night with Seth Meyers,” tipped her hand a little that the show was likely ending when she let slip that, “I was working on a short-lived but probably the best show ever, ‘The Hawk.’”

The cancellation comes in the wake of a changing of the guard at Netflix, with Peter Rice taking over for veteran creative chief Bela Bajaria. Our critic also recently wrote about the challenges Netflix is facing right now in getting audiences to discover new series in the ways they used to.

But at the same time, Netflix has already renewed a couple of other comedy series that even premiered after “The Hawk” later this year, including “Nobody Wants This,” “A Different World,” “Tires,” “Big Mistakes,” and “The Four Seasons.” Netflix is also still working with Ferrell’s Gloria Sanchez Productions on three new series, “All the Rage,” “Prep,” and another scripted series from Nikki Glaser.

Bruno Fernandes reveals what Mauricio Pochettino told Manchester United at half-time of Sporting draw

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Bruno Fernandes has revealed Mauricio Pochettino told Manchester United to be more aggressive at half-time of their dramatic draw with Sporting.

United went in at half-time trailing 2-0, but came back to draw 2-2 despite having a pair of goals controversially ruled out.

It was a dramatic affair in Lisbon, but it transpires Pochettino had some refreshingly simple advice at the break.

"[At half-time the manager] wanted more aggression. More bodies,” Fernandes explained. “The first goal comes from that.”

That first goal was product of a penalty won when Lisandro Martinez charged into the Sporting box.

Fernandes went on, saying United were frustrated not to have won: “We need points to get through. We need to focus on the next one. Now the moment is to get back to the Premier League. We need to stick together and fight for our lives.”

Senne Lammens endured a long evening, as he conceded the penalty which handed Sporting their second penalty of the day, from which the opener was scored, and dropped the ball to permit the hosts’ late equaliser.

But Fernandes was quick to put an arm around his keeper as he added to TNT Sports: “We gave a penalty away where Senne [Lammens] saved it. We gave one or two counters, but we were very good in recovery positions.

“Mistakes are part of football and from that you get goals. Everyone has to take the blame for everything we do good and we do bad as well.

“We need to be together in the good and in the bad moments.”

Hermoso: ‘Roma play in a special way, we have no fear’ after 2-2 with PSG

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Mario Hermoso explains how Roma’s unique approach under Gian Piero Gasperini saw defenders like him ‘bring something extra’ scoring goals in the 2-2 with PSG. ‘We play with no fear.’

The defender opened the scoring after just five minutes when coming forward to combine with Matias Soulé, then PSG turned it around through the usual free header on a corner by Illia Zabarnyi and the Ousmane Dembele penalty.

Donyell Malen converted his own spot-kick off the bench late on, after a Vitinha handling offence in the box, for the final 2-2 result.

“Everyone knows what the coach does, this is his identity, his style of playing,” Hermoso told Sky Sport Italia.

“We defenders get the opportunity to be protagonists, so I am very happy to come to a stadium like this, and see Roma playing in this special way, with no fear. We are on the right track and must keep taking these big steps forward.”

Mario Hermoso opens the scoring against PSG!

— Football on TNT Sports (@footballontnt) November 25, 2026

There were some very unexpected decisions from Gasperini in the starting XI, as he left Malen, Paulo Dybala and Bryan Cristante on the bench.

Perhaps this was a way of giving them something to come on during the match when the legs are beginning to tire from their man to man approach?

“Everyone in this squad is important, we are set out in a way that we can all be protagonists. Dony, Paulo and Bryan had more minutes in this season, but they came on with the right mentality, and were ready to help get a good result.”

Paris Saint-Germain only managed to surprise Roma from a corner and a penalty, so it was a strong overall performance from the Giallorossi.

“The coach gives you confidence to believe, to have this mentality of reacting to difficult moments that we suffered not just this season, but over the years. He brought this identity to Roma, and we are happy to carry it on for a great club like Roma.”

Hermoso also had a chance to score another goal from open play and got it wrong, so this is not a new experience for him.

“With these teammates who play in attack, we do try to make these moves in training sessions, everyone gets to have a go, and that means us defenders can bring something extra to the team, it’s an added benefit.

“I am also glad to see the young players coming through, we’ve got strong ideas on how to play, and I’m happy to be a part of it,” concluded the Spaniard.

Sporting v Man United LIVE: United winner disallowed by VAR in bonkers Champions League clash

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Sporting 2-2 Manchester United: A ridiculous Champions League clash finished all square after the visitors had two goals disallowed and conceded two penalties

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Mauricio Pochettino was denied his first win as Manchester United manager against Sporting as his side saw a late winner disallowed by VAR in a bonkers Champions league clash in Lisbon.

Referee Szymon Marciniak was in the thick of the action throughout and in the 84th minute ruled that Patrick Dorgu had fouled Sporting goalkeeper Kaique Pereira, who let a corner from Bruno Fernandes slip through his fingers.

United had only just conceded a late equaliser themselves when goalkeeper Senne Lammens fumbled a high cross and allowed Sporting striker Luis Suarez to hook in his second of the night.

A freak Fernandes goal had looked to have given United a lucky win as the captain, playing against his former team, saw his long-range shot take a wicked deflection off a divot in the pitch to beat the stranded goalkeeper.

It was a second goal for Fernandes in the space of three minutes as he levelled from the penalty spot, after Lisandro Martinez was brought down in the box.

United had trailed after a chaotic first half where they conceded two penalties in the space of three minutes, with Lammens saving the first from Suarez before conceding another 75 seconds later, with Suarez scoring at the second attempt.

Pochettino’s side were also aggrieved when Amad Diallo saw an equaliser disallowed for a foul in the build up, with Youri Tielemans instead penalised. Tielemans was booked, and was then sent off in stoppage time for picking up his second yellow card.

Follow all the latest updates from the Estadio Jose Alvalade:

Ally McCoist, who commented on the match for TNT Sports, reckons that Man Utd were unlucky not to take all three points from the game.

“Weighing everything up, I think he will be disappointed with a point,” he said.

“The first goal disallowed is a goal. I know Patrick Dorgu comes in, but the second goal disallowed should count as well. Manchester United were really unlucky.”

What does this result mean for the Champions League table?

Sporting CP are up to 9th and sit on 10 points. They’re one place outside the top eight and a place in the automatic qualifying spots.

Man United are still 19th with just seven points. They are two points above the elimination zone with three games still to play.

The best case scenario for them is to qualify for the play-off round and then beat whoever they’re drawn against.

In tonight’s other Champions League match featuring a Premier League side, Liverpool battered Club Brugge 3-0 away from home.

It caps off a brilliant week for Andoni Iraola and co who back up their victory over United with another in Europe.

The Sporting CP players look the more aggrieved at full time but I’d say they’ve been favoured more by the decisions made on this night.

United’s mistakes were their own and it’s cost them a first win under Pochettino.

Bruno Fernandes walks over for a chat with the officials to understand what’s been happening.

The players then head over to clap and applaud the United fans.

90+5 mins: United are under the pump for the final few minutes with one man fewer on the pitch.

The whistle goes to end this wild, wild match and the points are shared.

I wonder what Mauricio Pochettino thinks about all he’s seen here?

90+3 mins: Youri Tielemans is sent off having arrived late in a tackle.

He takes out the man, collects a second yellow card and is then dismissed.

90+2 mins: Bryan Mbeumo and Bruno Fernandes are taken off the pitch with Andrey Santos and Mason Mount brought on.

Fernandes is applauded by the Sporting fans given his history with the club.

90 mins: Five minutes of added time to play.

Pochettino is preparing another couple of subs...

87 mins: Man Utd have the ball up inside Sporting’s final third.

They can’t capitalise on it and Sporting look to attack on the break.

The cross comes to Araujo who shoots on the volley and smashes his effort wide of the far post!

The VAR takes a look at the incident and sends the referee to the pitchside monitor.

The officials decide that the goalkeeper was fouled in the build up and the goal doesn’t stand.

It’s a bizarre one as they cleared the knock on Lammens for Sporting’s equaliser.

Alejandro Jose Hernandez caused controversy as the man in the middle of Liverpool's 3-0 win at Club Brugge in the Champions League.

The Reds took an early lead as Alexander Isak continued his strong start to the season by tapping home Dominik Szoboszlai’s pass across the box.

Florian Wirtz doubled the visitors’ lead 11 minutes from time with a superb strike, while Isak grabbed his brace shortly after.

Liverpool are now fifth in the Champions League table with four wins from their first five league stage games.

Hernandez was not at his best as the referee of the Bruges clash on Wednesday evening.

Football Insider have picked out three decisions he clearly got wrong on the night.

Kerkez has built a reputation for rash tackling and being caught out of position throughout his short Liverpool career so far.

Wednesday night was no different as he took to body-checking Brugge winger Forbs in the 21st minute to stop him getting down the hosts' right flank.

Hernandez was lenient in his decision to only award a free-kick to Brugge, with the Reds full-back clearly getting away with one because it was still the first half.

The Spanish referee's mistake continued before the break as he got the simplest of decisions completely wrong.

Brugge playmaker Hugo Vetlesen struck a shot goalwards in the 29th minute that was clearly deflected behind for a corner by a visiting defender.

Hernandez somehow failed to spot the injury and kept play going for at least another 30 seconds before the ball found touch and home physios could come on to apply treatment.

Alexander Isak has put Liverpool 3–0 ahead at Club Brugge in their UEFA Champions League clash with the deftiest of finishes from a brilliant Alisson Becker assist.

With Liverpool leading by two-goals in the second half at the Jan Breydel Stadium, Alisson produced another trademark counter-attacking distribution in the 82nd minute.

UCL | Inter 2-0 VfB Stuttgart: Sucic and Lautaro Martinez revive hope

📅 Nov 25, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Inter managed to get the better of VfB Stuttgart and leapfrog them in the Champions League table thanks to Petar Sucic and Lautaro Martinez.

The Nerazzurri lost their first two Champions League games, then won back-to-back against Shakhtar Donetsk and Feyenoord. Hakan Calhanoglu and Ange-Yoan Bonny missed out, with Lautaro Martinez only fit for the bench after more than a month on the sidelines. Manuel Akanji returned in defence after Sunday’s chaotic 4-4 Serie A draw away to Atalanta.

VfB Stuttgart were one point in front of Inter going into this clash, with Angelo Stiller, Leo Sauer and Luca Jacquez back in the squad after injury.

It was an aggressive start from the Germans, as Maximilian Mittelstadt flashed a ball across the face of goal from the left, then Petar Sucic charged down the Ermedin Demirovic strike.

Josep Martinez needed a big save to deny the ferocious Chris Fuhrich strike at the near post, while Djed Spence wasted a very promising counter-attack from the centre-circle, firing straight at the on-rushing goalkeeper.

Inter threatened from a corner, flicked on by Yann Bisseck just beyond both Pio Esposito and Manuel Akanji, and another of the defender’s headers on a set play was charged down.

A well-worked move fell apart when Henrikh Mkhitaryan’s first touch let him down meeting the Marcus Thuram roll across from the left, while Ramon Hendriks had to make a perfect tackle on Spence inside the area.

Finishing from Bisseck and Sucic left something to be desired, but Inter were forced into a change when Alessandro Bastoni limped off with a left thigh injury. Federico Dimarco came on, moving Carlos Augusto to central defence.

The hosts had two huge chances to score in the space of three minutes. First, Marcus Thuram ran onto a misplaced pass only to drill it wide of the near post, then Barella’s curler off the outside of the boot bounced off the upright and Dennis Seimen clawed the rebound off the line.

Thuram also saw his strike deflected over the bar, but Nartey blasted just over from the edge of the area after the restart.

There was finally a breakthrough when Vagnoman charged down the Dimarco cross with a raised arm, spotted by VAR, and Petar Sucic kept his cool to convert the penalty.

VfB threatened from a corner kick, Bisseck getting himself in the way of the Nartey effort from point-blank range, while at the other end Dimarco’s deflected free kick was acrobatically palmed out from under the crossbar.

Carlos Augusto made a desperate interception to prevent Demirovic turning in at the back post, while Jamie Leweling’s curler was just over.

However, Inter doubled their lead when Pio Esposito let the Dimarco cross through with a dummy, allowing Lautaro Martinez to take a touch and fire in with the right foot.

Akanji also required a crucial block as Bilal El Khannouss burst into the box and got the shot away, while in stoppages Lautaro Martinez’s strike was charged down by a defender’s face.
